Entanglement structure for finite system under dual-unitary dynamics
Gaurav Rudra Malik, Rohit Kumar Shukla, Sudhanva Joshi, Aravinda S, Sunil Kumar Mishra

Abstract
The dynamics of quantum many-body systems in the chaotic regime are of particular interest due to the associated phenomena of information scrambling and entanglement generation within the system. While these systems are typically intractable using traditional numerical methods, an effective framework can be implemented based on dual-unitary circuits which have emerged as a minimal model for maximally chaotic dynamics. In this work, we investigate how individual two-body operators influence the global dynamics of circuits composed of dual-unitaries. We study their effect on entanglement generation while examining it from both bipartite and multipartite perspectives. Here we also highlight the significant role of local unitaries in the dynamics when paired with operators from the dual-unitary class, showing that systems with identical entangling power can exhibit a range of differing entanglement growth rates. Furthermore, we present calculations establishing time-step-dependent lower bounds, which depend on both the initial state and the entangling power of the constituent operators. Finally, we find that time-evolving an initial state composed of pair products generates a state with nearly maximal multipartite entanglement content, approaching the bounds established by Absolutely Maximally Entangled (AME) states.



I
Introduction

Entanglement is a hallmark of quantum many-body systems and serves as a central resource in quantum information processing
Horodecki et al. 2009
;
Bennett and DiVincenzo 2000
;
Gottesman and Chuang 1999
;
Ekert and Jozsa 1996
;
Jennewein et al. 2000
while also indicating important features within the system at hand, such as, the area law dependence which indicates the presence of a gapped spectrum
Eisert et al. 2010
. It also has further implications for the numerical simulation of a given system via the applicability of tensor networks
Orús 2014
;
Schollwöck 2011
.

The dynamical nature of the system is also reflected in the generation of entanglement under time evolution. It is known that most quantum systems, in the absence of specific features, dynamically converge to the thermal state under unitary time evolution
Nandkishore and Huse 2015
. Here the initial specifications of the system are suppressed and the quantum observables may be accurately estimated using ensembles of random matrices
Alhambra 2023
. A feature associated with the dynamics of a many-body system is information scrambling, which is observed in the presence of some dynamical complexity such as that brought on by nonintegrability
Swingle et al. 2016
;
Prosen 1999
;
Prosen 2007
. Apart from scrambling effects, nonintegrability also plays a prominent role in localization to thermalization transitions
Abanin et al. 2019
;
Kjäll et al. 2014
. There exist several metrics to quantify the scrambling of information within a system such as the bipartite mutual information (BMI), tripartite mutual information (TMI)
Iyoda and Sagawa 2018
and the out-of-time-order correlation (OTOC)
Lin and Motrunich 2018
;
Shukla et al. 2022
. The OTOC shows exponential dependence with time for quantum chaotic systems associated with an infinite Hilbert space and is directly related to the quantum Lyapunov exponent
Maldacena et al. 2016
.

For nonintegrable systems having local interactions, unitary time evolution can be effectively simulated using random unitary quantum circuits and the associated growth of entanglement across equal bipartitions shows a linear dependence on time
Nahum et al. 2018
;
Nahum et al. 2017
;
Khemani et al. 2018
;
von Keyserlingk et al. 2018
. The connection between dynamic complexity and entanglement growth is further substantiated with quantum chaotic time evolution leading to states with near-maximum entanglement, starting from a completely separable state
Bandyopadhyay and Lakshminarayan 2002
;
Miller and Sarkar 1999
. Apart from state dependence, an analysis of the involved complexity can be made by studying the operators bringing about the said time evolution. The associated state-independent and local unitary invariant quantities include operator entanglement
Zanardi 2001
and entangling power
Zanardi et al. 2000
. The latter plays a significant role in the dynamic properties of the system and can be effectively used to construct an ergodic hierarchy of quantum evolutions based on the rate of information scrambling
Aravinda et al. 2021
, while the former has been shown to be intricately linked to the OTOC
Styliaris et al. 2021
.

While the nonintegrable unitary evolution generates a rich set of physical features, their numerical simulations are challenging as the high rate of entanglement generation restricts the application of dimensionality reduction used in a tensor network approach. However, it is possible to obtain results for complex dynamics by means of dual-unitary operators, which serve as minimal models of generating quantum chaotic evolutions
Bruno Bertini 2025
. This already includes the spatiotemporal correlation functions
Bertini et al. 2019a
, the OTOC
Claeys and Lamacraft 2020
and entanglement membranes
Rampp et al. 2024
. The full state dynamics can also be obtained for the class of solvable states
Piroli et al. 2020
, while the bipartite entanglement features can also be computed for a more generic class of states
Foligno and Bertini 2023
. The computational power of the said circuits may be used for a quantum advantage
Suzuki et al. 2022
and elements of ergodicity breaking are also observed when starting out from scar states
Logarić et al. 2024
.

In our present work we consider the dynamics resulting from a finite width brickwall quantum circuit made up of dual-unitary operators acting on a generic initial state. Our motivation is to understand the manner in which an individual operator used in constructing the brickwall circuit influences the overall dynamics, particularly in reference to the entanglement structure generated within the system. In this regard, the entangling power of the single operator plays an important role with respect to both the bipartite and multipartite entanglement measures. We also consider the role of local operators, in terms of random single qubit unitaries, for the bipartite case which is known to affect the entanglement generating capabilities of a circuit
Jonnadula et al. 2017
.

For identifying the role of local dynamics we consider an ensemble of circuits where each member is composed of a repeating pattern of a single operator. Between members of the ensemble we use operators that are different, albeit local unitary equivalent. In analysing the results obtained, we establish the role of a single two-body operator in the overall system dynamics for a more generic case, left ambiguous by the entangling power. Our results also highlight the initial buildup of entanglement within a circuit of finite spatial width and temporal depth, and agree well with the said dynamics for an infinite system in both space and time
Foligno and Bertini 2023
. Further, while there have been several studies based on the entanglement associated with bipartitions, not a lot of literature exists when considering multipartite entanglement
Pal and Lakshminarayan 2018
;
Lakshminarayan and Subrahmanyam 2005
. In our results we again find a strong dependence of the entangling power of the individual gates and multipartite features of entanglement with respect to the measure of time-evolved state and also of the entire circuit. Overall we find that the entanglement generation is favored by chaotic dynamics, for both the bipartite and multipartite cases, similar to that in the semiclassical regime
Wang et al. 2004
.

This manuscript is organized as follows: In Section
II
we discuss dual-unitary quantum circuits and the measures of mixing rate, its dependence on entangling power and the relationship with ergodic hierarchy. Section
III
presents our results on bipartite entanglement growth, with a focus on the roles of mixing rate and entangling power. In Section
IV
we examine multipartite entanglement in the time-evolved state and characterize the circuit properties responsible for the observed entanglement structure. Comparative results for spin models are discussed in Section
V
, and we conclude with a summary of our findings in Section
VI
.

II
Setup

II.1
Dual Unitary Operators and Initial State

The quantum circuit central to our discussion is composed of unitary operators
U
acting on two qudits of local dimension
q
, specifically from the class of dual-unitary operators (
\mathcal{DU}(q)
). A unitary operator
U\in\mathcal{DU}(q)
if in addition to satisfying the standard unitary condition (
UU^{\dagger}=U^{\dagger}U=I
), its associated dual operator
\tilde{U}
is also unitary. The two-body operator can be written as a rank-4 tensor as
U_{ijkl}
, where each index can take
q
values
\{0,1,2,...q-1\}
. The particular element
U_{ijkl}
is given by the expression:

U_{ijkl}=\bra{ij}U\ket{kl}

(1)

The dual operator
\tilde{U}
is obtained by a specific rearrangement of the indices of the original operator tensor, known as the
R_{1}/R_{2}
transformation. This operation, along with that partial transposition are defined via exchange transformations given by:

•

R_{1}
realignment:
\bra{ij}U\ket{kl}=\bra{lj}U^{R_{1}}\ket{ki}

•

R_{2}
realignment:
\bra{ij}U\ket{kl}=\bra{ik}U^{R_{2}}\ket{jl}

•

T_{1}
partial transpose:
\bra{ij}U\ket{kl}=\bra{kj}U^{T_{1}}\ket{il}

•

T_{2}
partial transpose:
\bra{ij}U\ket{kl}=\bra{il}U^{T_{2}}\ket{jk}

These definitions are useful from a notational perspective when considering dual-unitaries. We may now formally define
\mathcal{DU}(q)
as:

\mathcal{DU}(q)=\{U\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q})\ |\ U^{R}(U^{R})^{\dagger}=\mathbb{I}=(U^{R})^{\dagger}U^{R}\}

(2)

Here,
R
maybe taken as either
R_{1}
or
R_{2}
, and
\mathcal{L}(V)
represents all the unitary operators acting on the vector space
V
. Finally,
\mathcal{H}_{q}
represents the Hilbert space of local dimension
q
. The dual operator
\tilde{U}
is given as
U^{R}
. Moreover, there exists another class of operators called as 2-unitaries,
\mathcal{U}_{2}(q)
given by:

\mathcal{U}_{2}(q)=\{U\in\mathcal{DU}(q)\ |\ U^{T}(U^{T})^{\dagger}=\mathbb{I}=(U^{T})^{\dagger}U^{T}\}

(3)

here,
T
maybe taken as either
T_{1}
or
T_{2}
. Clearly,
\mathcal{U}_{2}(q)\subset\mathcal{DU}(q)\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q})
. For an operator
U\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q})
, the implementation complexity depends on its non-locality which is estimated following the Schmidt decomposition of the operator into operators on constituent vector spaces:

U=\sum_{j=0}^{q^{2}-1}\sqrt{\gamma_{j}}X_{j}\otimes Y_{j}\,\,\,\,\,,

(4)

where the operators
X_{k}
and
Y_{k}

\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{q})
form the orthonormal basis under the Hilbert-Schmidt (HS) product
\bra{A_i}A_{j}\rangle=\tr(A_i^\dagger A_j)=\delta_{ij}
. This follows as the space
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{q})
along with the HS product itself forms a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{q}_{HS}
, which is isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}_{q}^{\otimes 2}
. Following this, the entanglement within operator
U
is given as:

E(U)=1-\frac{1}{q^{4}}\sum_{j=0}^{q^{2}-1}\gamma_{j}^{2}\,\,\,\,\,.

(5)

In order to address the non-local and entangling nature of the classes
\mathcal{DU}(q)
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}(q)
from a state perspective, we may associate the unitary bipartite operation
U
to the four party state
\ket{\psi_{PQRS}}
via the Choi-Jamiolkowski isomorphism between
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathcal{H}
, defined as
Bengtsson and Zyczkowski 2006
:

\ket{\psi_{PQRS}}=(U_{PQ}\otimes\mathbb{I}_{RS})\ket{\phi_{PR}^{+}}\otimes\ket{\phi_{QS}^{+}}\,\,\,\,\,.

(6)

This state is maximally entangled across the bipartition
PQ|RS
owing to the unitary nature of the matrix
U
. For a unitary matrix
U\in\mathcal{DU}(q)
, maximal entanglement additionally exists for the bipartition
PR|QS
and also
PS|RQ
if
U\in\mathcal{U}_{2}(q)

Jonnadula et al. 2020
making
\ket{\psi_{PQRS}}
an absolutely maximally entangled (AME) state
Helwig et al. 2012
. While the AME states donot exist for arbitrary number of parties and local dimensions
Rather et al. 2022
, as for a four-party qubit state
Higuchi and Sudbery 2000
, examples do exist for local dimension
q=3
with the same number of qutrits. This implies that
\mathcal{U}_{2}(2)=\emptyset
while
\mathcal{U}_{2}(3)\neq\emptyset
following the correspondence established by Eq.
6
. The maximum entanglement across the bipartition
PR|QS
for
\ket{\psi_{PQRS}}
also implies maximum operator entanglement
\forall U\in\mathcal{DU}(q)
as the reduced state
\rho_{PR}=U^{R_{1}}(U^{R_{1}})^{\dagger}/q^{2}
. With linearized entropy as the entanglement measure,

E(U)=1-\tr({\rho_{PR}^2})=1-\frac{1}{q^{4}}\tr\Big( \Big[U^{R_1}(U^{R_1})^{\dagger} \Big]^2 \Big).

(7)

Thus, by definition of
\mathcal{DU}(q)
and
\tr({\mathbb{I_q}})=q^{2}
we have:

E(U)=max_{U}E(U)\Big(=E(S_{q})\Big)=1-\frac{1}{q^{2}},\forall\ U\in\mathcal{DU}(q)

(8)

here
S_{q}
is the SWAP operator for local dimension
q
. In context of the
PS|RQ
bipartition, the reduced state is given by
\rho_{PS}=U^{T_{2}}(U^{T_{2}})^{\dagger}/q^{2}
, which is same as
E(US)
. This can be understoond by the action of
S
along with the operator
U
which leads to the state
\ket{\psi_{QPRS}}
following Eq.(
6
). Now, as
E(U)
is given by
E(\ket{\psi_{PQRS}})_{PR|QS}
, it follows that
E(US)=E(\ket{\psi_{QPRS}}_{QR|PS})
and is the same as the expression obtained using the partial transpose operation
T

Jonnadula et al. 2020
;
Ma and Wang 2007
. Hence we have
\forall U\in\mathcal{U}_{2}(q)
,

E(U)=E(US_{q})=E(S_{q})\,\,\,\,\,,

(9)

which in turn maximizes the entangling power
e_{P}(U)
i.e. the average bipartite entanglement generated when an ensemble of product initial states is acted upon by the operator
U
. Therefore by definition:

e_{P}(U)\propto\ \mathbb{E}_{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}}\Big[E\Big(U\ (\ket{\psi_1}\otimes\ket{\psi_2})\Big)\Big].

(10)

This expression is an ensemble average which also has a closed form arising due to the unitary invariance of the Haar measure and group theoretic arguments
Zanardi et al. 2000
;
Wang and Zanardi 2002
. The same expression can also be had using an explicit linear algebra calculation
Mele 2024
.

e_{P}(U)=C_{q}\Big(E(U)+E(US)-E(S)\Big).

(11)

Here the factor
C_{q}=q^{2}/(q+1)^{2}
, implying that the maximum value that can be reached for
e_{P}(U)
is
q^{2}E(S)/(q+1)^{2}
when Eq.
9
is satisfied. This leads to
e_{p}(U)=(q-1)/(q+1)
using Eq. (
8
)
\forall U\in\mathcal{U}_{2}(q)

Zanardi 2001
.

In order to scale the values such that
0\leq e_{P}(U)\leq 1
, we divide the expression Eq. (
11
) by the maximum
e_{P}(U)
which also leads to the pre-factor taking the form
1/E(S)
. This defines the measure of
e_{P}(U)
that we use within the manuscript. Thus, for the set
\mathcal{U}_{2}(q)
the value of
e_{P}(U)
is unity corresponding to the existence of the AME(4,3) state. Since the AME(4,2) state does not exist, a qubit operator cannot be 2-unitary, and therefore the entangling power cannot attain the value of 1. For qubits, the entangling power if therefore limited
Kraus and Cirac 2001
and can attain a maximum value of
2/9

Balakrishnan and Sankaranarayanan 2010
, which following normalization is restricted to
2/3
.

Now using graphical notation to represent the circuit, we introduce the tensor form for a single local gate
U
and its hermitian conjugate
U^{\dagger}
:

U=
,

U^{\dagger}=
.

(12)

Here the four free legs indicate the free indices, that maybe contracted over to specify the unitary relations. We begin with primary unitary condition, given by
UU^{\dagger}=U^{\dagger}U=\mathbb{I}
, valid
\forall\ U\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}_{q}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q})
:

(15)

These unitary relations enable the efficient calculation of quantities like the spatio-temporal correlation functions
Bertini et al. 2019a
and the OTOC
Claeys and Lamacraft 2020
. For our calculations, we take examples of dual-unitaries
\in\mathcal{DU}(2)
using the Cartan decomposition of 2-qubit gates
Kraus and Cirac 2001
, and for the qutrit case we obtain elements
\in\mathcal{DU}(3)
using the approach described in
Rather et al. 2020
(see Appendix
A.1
and
A.2
).

We now describe the structure of our finite quantum circuit consisting of
L
(= even) qudits where the associated time evolution takes place via a brickwall style arrangement of individual
U\in\mathcal{DU}(q)
. Using the tensor notation introduced previously, a single time-step of the brickwall circuit as
\mathcal{U}
is defined as:

\mathcal{U}=\Big(\otimes_{i\in Z_{even}}U^{i,i+1}.\otimes_{j\in Z_{odd}}U^{j,j+1}\Big).

(16)

Here
U^{pq}
represents the operator
U
acting on the
pth
and
qth
lattice site. In our numbering convention we assign integer values moving from left to right, starting with
0
for the left-most index. Also, periodic boundary conditions are assumed, implying the
L
th index is the same as the
0
th index. Using the graphical notation specified in Eq.
12
we therefore have
\mathcal{U}
for
L=12
:

\mathcal{U}=\hbox to219.03pt{\vbox to46.22pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 86.9242pt\lower-20.21272pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(17)

For a total of
t
time steps, the total time evolution operator is thereby given as:
\mathbb{U}(t)=\mathcal{U}^{t}
, which comprises
2t
layers of gates with our numbering convention. This also implies that every time step gives an even integer value along the
t
axis. The remaining parameter is that of the initial wavefunction, and for this purpose we consider the generic pair product state, which is represented as the following tensor product:

|\psi\rangle_{initial}=\frac{1}{q^{N/2}}\Big(\sum_{i,j=0}^{q-1}m_{ij}|ij\rangle\Big)^{\otimes N}\,\,\,\,\,,

(18)

where
N=L/2
specifies the number of pair products. The coefficients
m_{ij}
of a given pair product itself form a rank-2 tensor, along with its conjugate, with the constraint
\tr(m m^{\dagger})=d
applied for normalisation. The generic matrix
m
leads up to a class of states which do not admit any specific feature, and in general is taken to be a matrix with random entries in this manuscript.

(20)

Thus the time evolution within our system is represented by a repeating segment of the operator
\mathcal{U}
with the lower most indices being plugged by the tensor
\ket{\psi_{init}}
to leave no open index other than those on the other end of the tensor structure, representing the time evolved state. For this, the index labeled as
k
within the state tensor in Eq.
20
is contracted with the correspondingly labeled index
k
within the operator tensor in Eq.
17
. For the final index, we use PBC, and instead contract with the
0
index. Once the time evolved state is obtained, we can calculate the quantities of interest.

Our primary objective is to consider the effect of local operators
U
on the global dynamics generated by the circuit
\mathbb{U}(t)
. The quantity of interest therefore is the entangling power
e_{P}(U)
. However using this metric it is not possible to account for local unitaries. To remedy this we take an ensemble of
U^{\prime}
given by:

U^{\prime}=(u_{1}\otimes u_{2})U(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}),\,\ u_{i},v_{j}\in Haar,\,\,i,j=\{1,2\}

(21)

for a specified
U
having fixed entangling power, drawing the local unitary operators
u
and
v
from the Haar random ensemble. Note that
U^{\prime}\in\mathcal{DU}(q)
, when
U\in\mathcal{DU}(q)
. This results in an ensemble of circuits
\{\mathbb{U}(t)\}_{U^{\prime}}
for which the mixing rate is a suitable metric to study the dynamics of our system. In a following section we shall denote
U^{\prime}
with a different colour than Eq.
12
.

II.2
Ergodic Hierarchy and Mixing Rate

The correlation function between two space-time points in a
1+1D
circuit composed of unitaries leads to a non-zero contribution iff the points lie within the light cone connecting them
Chan et al. 2018
. For the case where
U\in\mathcal{DU}(q)
, the said correlations give a non-zero contribution only if the points lie exactly on the light cone and show decaying, oscillating or constant behavior with time. Further, for
U\in\mathcal{U}_{2}(q)
this correlation also vanishes, and non-zero behavior is found only in correlating a space-time point with itself. This range of dynamical behaviors for the correlation function for
U\in\mathcal{DU}(q)
is therefore indicative of an ergodic hierarchy with the class of
\mathcal{U}_{2}(q)
operators at the apex.

Using the notation from Ref.
Bertini et al. 2019a
, the correlation function on the light cone for
U\in\mathcal{DU}(q)
is given as:

C^{\alpha\beta}_{\nu}(x=t,t)=\frac{1}{q}\tr[\mathcal{M}_{\nu}^{2t}(a^{\beta})a^{\alpha}].

(22)

x=t
indicates the correlation function is evaluated at the light-cone
x=vt
(
v=1
if
U\in\mathcal{DU}(q)

Claeys and Lamacraft 2020
) with
\nu=\pm
indicating the direction of light-cone. Here
a^{\alpha/\beta}
denote the local observables. Thus the time dependence and the role of the operator both appear with the term
\mathcal{M}_{\pm}
, which can be defined for
\nu=+
as follows:

\mathcal{M}_{+}(a)=\frac{1}{q}\tr_{1}\Big[U^{\dagger}(a\otimes\mathbb{I})U\Big]=\hbox to46.25pt{\vbox to86.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 31.62589pt\lower-43.11726pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
\par{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.84 -9.84 C 12.47 -2.63 12.47 2.63 9.84 9.84}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -9.84 -9.84 C -12.47 -2.63 -12.47 2.63 -9.84 9.84}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -19.69 39.37 C -43.21 59.11 -43.21 -59.11 -19.69 -39.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(23)

Clearly,
\mathcal{M}_{+}
is a unital channel that forms a linear map between an initial
\rho_{i}
and final density matrix
\rho_{f}
, such that
\rho_{f}=\mathcal{M}_{\pm}(\rho_{i})
. The said action can be modified following row vectorization as
M_{\pm}(U)
acting on
\ket{\rho_i}
, which to give
\ket{\rho_f}
, such that
\ket{\rho_f}=M_{\pm}(U)\ket{\rho_i}
, where
\ket{\rho_{i/f}}
are column vectors with
q^{2}
entries. Following algebraic manipulation, we can also write the operators
M_{+}(U)
in terms of matrix rearrangements
Aravinda et al. 2021

M_{+}(U)=\frac{1}{q}\Big[U^{T_{2}}(U^{T_{2}})^{\dagger}\Big]^{R_{1}}.

(24)

For
\nu
being
-ve
,
\mathcal{M}_{-}(a)=\frac{1}{q}\tr_{2}\Big[U^{\dagger}(\mathbb{I}\otimes a)U\Big]
, with
M_{-}(U)=\frac{1}{q}\Big[U^{T_{2}}(U^{T_{2}})^{\dagger}\Big]^{R_{2}}
upon row vectorization.

Figure 1:
The variation of
\lambda_{1}
with the entangling power
e_{P}(U)
, for an ensemble of 10000
U^{\prime}
operators created using Haar random local unitaries for qubits (
a
and
b
) and qutrits (
c
and
d
). In
(a)
and
(c)
we plot the real and imaginary parts of
\lambda_{1}
for each member of the ensemble and in
(b)
and
(d)
we plot their magnitude, with the dotted line representing the average.

The nature of the correlation function hence depends strongly on the properties of eigenvalues of
M_{\pm}(U)
, as a spectral decomposition of this matrix reveal the constant, decaying and oscillating modes. This in turn can be used to specify the ergodic hierarchy. Being unital in nature, one of the eigenvalues is certain to be unity, and forms a trivial case. We denote this eigenvalue as
\lambda_{0}
, and arrange the others for
M_{+}(U)
(or
M_{-}(U)
instead) in an increasing order based on their magnitude. This implies that
|\lambda_{q^{2}-1}|\leq|\lambda_{q^{2}-2}|\leq...\leq|\lambda_{2}|\leq|\lambda_{1}|\leq|\lambda_{0}|\ (=1)
. The ergodic hierarchy is defined as follows:

•

Noninteracting
: all
(q^{2}-1)
eigenvalues are
=1
. Constant correlations are obtained, an example is that of the
S_{q}
gate.

•

Nonergodic
(Interacting and nonintegrable):
\exists k
, s.t.
\forall j>k\in\{1,2,...,q^{2}-1\}
,
\lambda_{j}
is non-trivial. Implies that
\lambda_{l}=1\ \forall\ l\geq k
, i.e.
k
constant modes are present.

•

Ergodic but nonmixing
:
\lambda_{k}\neq 1\forall\ k\in\{1,2,...q^{2}-1\}
. However,
\exists j\in\{1,2,...q^{2}-1\}
s.t.
|\lambda_{j}|=1
. This condition precludes mixing in the induced dynamics.

•

Ergodic and mixing
: All possible
(q^{2}-1)
eigenvalues are within the unit circle, i.e.
|\lambda_{k}|<1,\forall k\in\{1,2,...q^{2}-1\}
.

•

Bernoulli
: All
(q^{2}-1)
eigenvalues have numerical value
=0
.

Thus, assuming the ordering of eigenvalues according to their magnitude, the dynamical class in which a system belongs to can be judged by the value of
|\lambda_{1}|
, i.e. the largest non-trivial eigenvalue. Correspondingly we can also define an associated quantity called as the mixing rate
\mu_{1}=-\log|\lambda_{1}|
which directly corresponds to the ergodic nature of the system, being
0
for the Non-Interacting class and going to
\infty
for Bernoulli.

Here it is important to note that the spectrum of
M_{\pm}(U)
is not invariant upon the action of local unitaries. Infact for
U^{\prime}=(u_{1}\otimes u_{2})U(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})
, we have:

M_{+}(U^{\prime})=(v_{2}^{\dagger}\otimes v_{2}^{T})M_{+}(U)(u_{1}^{\dagger}\otimes u_{1}^{T}).

(25)

Yet, the norm of
M_{\pm}(U)
is constrained by the local unitary invariant of
e_{P}(U)
as

||M_{+}(U^{\prime})||^{2}=(q^{2}-1)(1-e_{P}(U))+1,

(26)

Thus for a given
U
with a fixed value of
e_{P}(U)
, an ensemble of
U^{\prime}
operators may each have different values of the mixing rate. Further in context of Eq.
26
increasing the entangling power lowers the total sum of all eigenvalues, which tends to lower the value of
|\lambda_{1}|
. However, the distribution of
\lambda
values is independent, leading to the ensemble having a wide range of mixing rates for a given value of
e_{P}(U)
.
For the dual-circuit to be guaranteed as mixing, (i.e.
\absolutevalue{\lambda_1}<1
irrespective of any random operators) the entangling power of the unitary brick
U
must satisfy
Aravinda et al. 2021
:

e_{P}(U)>e_{P}^{*}(U)=\frac{q^{2}-2}{q^{2}-1}.

(27)

In figures Fig.
1
(a),(c)
we plot the real and imaginary part of
\lambda_{1}
for an ensemble of local unitary equivalent operators and the range of magnitude obtained by the same for qubits and qutrits respectively in Fig.
1
(b),(d)
. We observe that for increasing values of
e_{P}(U)
, the ring formed by the real and imaginary parts of
\lambda_{1}
becomes smaller, indicating diminished magnitudes of
\lambda_{1}
, which reduces to a point for the case when
e_{P}(U)=1
in the qutrit case. This reflects the Bernoulli ergodic class corresponding to
|\lambda_{i}|=0\forall i
. Moreover, upon increasing the entangling power
e_{P}(U)
the ensemble average of
\lambda_{1}
get diminished, reflected in the lower reaches of
\lambda_{1}
on increasing
e_{P}(U)
. Since
|\lambda_{1}|
is the largest not trivial eigenvalue of the operator
M_{+}
, it maybe represented as the spectral radius, i.e. the largest magnitude eigenvalue, of an associated operator
\tilde{M}_{+}
, given as:

\tilde{M}_{+}=M_{+}-\ket{\phi}\bra{\phi}\,\,\,\,\,,

(28)

where
\ket{\phi}
represents the eigenvector of
M
having the trivial eigenvalue of
\lambda_{0}=1
. The eigen spectrum of the operator
\tilde{M}_{+}
is given as
\{0,\lambda_{q^{2}-1},\lambda_{q^{2}-2},...,\lambda_{1}\}
with the spectral radius for
\tilde{M}_{+}
via Gelfand’s formula being:

|\lambda_{1}|=lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}||\tilde{M}_{+}^{k}||_{HS}^{1/k}\,\,\,\,\,,

(29)

where
||\tilde{M}_{+}^{k}||_{HS}
represents the operator norm of
\tilde{M}_{+}
. Thus, the average mixing rate on the Haar random measure is given as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{u_{i},v_{i}}|\lambda_{1}|=\int_{Haar}du\ dv\lambda_{1}\Big((u_{1}\otimes u_{2})U(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})\Big)

(30)

\displaystyle=\int_{Haar}du\ dv\ \lambda_{1}\Big((u_{1}*v_{1}\otimes u_{2}*v_{2})U\Big)

(31)

\displaystyle=\int_{Haar}dw\lambda_{1}\Big((w\otimes w^{*})U\Big),

(32)

using Eq.
29
, we can equivalently write:

\mathbb{E}_{u_{i},v_{i}}|\lambda_{1}|=lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{Haar}dw\lambda_{1}\Big(||(w\otimes w^{*})U^{k}||_{HS}^{1/k}\Big).

(33)

The Hilbert-Schmidt norm provides a bound for the magnitude of
|\lambda_{1}|
. Moreover the numerical value of
\overline{||\tilde{M}_{+}^{k}||}_{HS}
is approximated as
Aravinda et al. 2021
:

\overline{||\tilde{M}_{+}^{k}||}_{HS}\approx(1-q^{2})(1-e_{P}(U))^{k}\,\,\,\,\,.

(34)

Figure 2:
(a)
Variation for the average mixing rate
\mathbb{E}_{u_{i},v_{i}}|\lambda_{1}|
with entangling power
e_{P}(U)
for the qubit and qutrit case, along with their respective fudge factors.
(b)
Variation of dispersion in
|\lambda_{1}|
with
e_{P}(U)

Using equation Eq.
29
, we can define an approximate bound for
\mathbb{E}_{u_{i},v_{i}}|\lambda_{1}|
, with the help of a fudge factor
f
to represent this relation.

\mathbb{E}_{u_{i},v_{i}}|\lambda_{1}|\approx f\sqrt{1-e_{P}(U})\,\,.

(35)

The fudge factor clearly differentiates
e_{P}(U)
from the average mixing rate
\mathbb{E}_{u_{i},v_{i}}(\mu_{1})=-\ln\Big( \mathbb{E}_{u_i,v_i} |\lambda_1| \Big)
while establishing the approximate relation between the two. This fudge factor can be found by numerically fitting the values of
\mathbb{E}_{u_{i},v_{i}}
with
\sqrt{1-e_{P}(U)}
. For the case of qubits, defined via the Cartan form of dual unitaries there is a uniform fudge factor
throughout the entire range of entangling powers. However, for the qutrit case where the operators are obtained via the
\mathcal{M}_{R}
algorithm, different fudge factors are required for the higher and lower sections of
e_{P}(U)
to minimize deviations (see
2

(a)
). This highlights that while
|\lambda_{1}|
certainly depends on
e_{P}(U)
, it is not a quantity that can be derived from it. Another feature that characterizes the relationship between mixing rate and entangling power is the relative dispersion of
|\lambda_{1}|

across different values of
e_{P}(U)
. We observe that lower entangling power
e_{P}(U)
produces eigenvalues
|\lambda_{1}|
with higher magnitudes and smaller variability, while higher
e_{P}(U)

leads to lower magnitudes with greater spread. To quantify this behavior, we define the relative dispersion measure:

\frac{R}{M}\Big(|\lambda_{1}|\Big)=\frac{\text{range}(|\lambda_{1}|)}{\overline{|\lambda_{1}|}}.

(36)

This ratio increases as we move toward higher entangling power
e_{P}(U)
, primarily due to the decreasing mean value
\overline{|\lambda_{1}|}
in the denominator. Since mixing rate is directly dependent on the value
|\lambda_{1}|
we use the latter to describe the ergodic nature of the evolution.

In the following section we shall provide results relating the entanglement dynamics with the mixing rate within the system, which is an important observation given that the definition of channel
\mathcal{M}_{+}
leading to the matrix
M_{+}
and its largest non-trivial eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
is in effect for an infinite temperature state at the thermodynamic limit. Yet we find that it has a discernible effect on the dynamics of a finite system with an arbitrary initial state. It therefore indicates mixing rate to be a fundamental feature related to the system dynamics.

III
Bipartite Entanglement Velocity

The entanglement growth within a system can be defined by the metric of entanglement velocity
Foligno and Bertini 2023
. Consider a subsystem denoted by
A
, comprising a series of
n
contiguous qudits having indices:
i,i+1,...j
. The entanglement of this block with the remaining system
\bar{A}
at a time step
t
can be obtained by the entropy measures of the associated partial density matrix obtained after tracing out
\bar{A}
, with
\mathbb{U}(t)\in\{\mathbb{U}(t)\}_{U^{\prime}}
:

\rho_{A}(t)=\tr_{\bar{A}}\Big(\mathbb{U}(t)\ket{\psi_{init}}\bra{\psi_{init}}\mathbb{U}^{\dagger}(t)\Big).

(37)

The corresponding Renyi entropy of an order
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
can be calculated by:

S_{A}^{\alpha}(t)=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\ln\Big( \tr(\rho_A^{\alpha}(t)) \Big).

(38)

To implement the expression
\rho_{A}^{\alpha}(t)
by the graphical approach we proceed by drawing out the complete tensor diagram corresponding to
\ket{\psi(t)}=\mathbb{U}(t)\ket{\psi_{init}}
, and also for
\bra{\psi(t)}=\bra{\psi_{init}}\mathbb{U}(t)
using conjugate elements. The partial trace can then be obtained by contracting indices corresponding to the subsystem
\bar{A}
, where using the unitary relations we can simplify the diagram. Taking powers of the resulting network, as required in calculating the Renyi entropy, the diagram factorises in terms of the initial state dependent
C_{x}
matrix (see Appendix
B.1
) for a few initial time steps
t<t^{*}
(
t^{*}
is dependent on system size). The explicit tensor representation of
C_{x}
(involving a particular
U^{\prime}
) is given as:

C_{x}=\frac{1}{q^{x/2}}\hbox to162.98pt{\vbox to83.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.47182pt\lower-6.37509pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(39)

The constant
1/q^{x/2}
comes from the initial state where
1/\sqrt{q}
is the normalisation for each of the involved
x
number of pair products. The total open indices on the left and right hand sides of the tensor
C_{x}
equals
2x
, implying that
C_{x}
is matrix of order
q^{x}*q^{x}
. For simplicity we consider a half-half bipartition including
L/2
qudits, such that
i=L/4
and
j=L-i-1
. For this construct, at a time
t
the resulting
C
operator is
C_{2t}
(
C_{2t+1}
) if
i
is even (odd) factorising the expression for
\rho^{\alpha}(t)
as
Foligno and Bertini 2023
:

\rho^{\alpha}(t)=\tr[(C_{\kappa_{t}}^{\dagger}C_{\kappa_{t}})^{\alpha}]\tr[(C_{\kappa_{t}}^{\dagger}C_{\kappa_{t}})^{\alpha}],

(40)

with
\kappa_{t}
being the appropriate subscript for the operator
C
. For
L
being an even number other than the multiple of
4
, the factors in Eq.
40
would be different from each other. Also, for the factorisation to take place, the
C
operators must not overlap, and this condition is given by
t<t^{*}
, where
t^{*}
satisfies the constraint
2\kappa_{t^{*}}=N
. This can be observed as an operator
C_{x}
has
x
pair products, and at
t^{*}
the operators are end-to-end, implying the constraint for
N
number of total pair products. It indicates that
t^{*}=N/4
or
N/4-1/2
for
L
being an even or odd multiple of
4
. Thus, we have:

S^{\alpha}_{A}(t)=\frac{2}{1-\alpha}\ln\Big( \tr(C_{\kappa_t}C_{\kappa_t}^{\dagger}) \Big).

(41)

Figure 3:
Build up of entanglement with time in the finite system for increasing sizes.
(a)
&
(b)
denote the qubit case while
(c)
&
(d)
are for qutrits.
(a)
and
(c)
denotes initial state with unitary
m
and arbitrary entangling powers of
U
, while
(b)
and
(d)
are for a random initial state, with
e_{P}(U)=0.61
and
e_{P}(U)=0.86
respectively.

Using the expression for
C_{x}
, it can be verified that for all cases
\tr(C_{x}C_{x}^{\dagger})=1
, with
\lambda_{i}
being eigenvalues of
C_{x}C_{x}^{\dagger}
. With the constraint
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}=1
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(q^{x})^{\alpha-1}}\leq\tr( (C_x C_x^{\dagger})^{\alpha})\leq 1,

(42)

\displaystyle\implies 2x\ln q\geq\frac{2\ln\tr( (C_x C_x^{\dagger})^{\alpha})}{1-\alpha}\geq 0,

(43)

where the left (right) inequality is obtained for
\lambda_{i}=1/q^{x}\,\forall i
(
\lambda_{i}=1
, for some
i
). For the maximal case, the rate of entanglement growth coincides to the maximum value for all orders
\alpha

Bertini et al. 2019b
. Entanglement velocity can then be described as a fraction of the maximum possible entanglement generated as a function of
x
, given by:

v_{E}^{(\alpha)}(x)=\frac{2\ln\tr( (C_x C_x^{\dagger})^{\alpha})/(1-\alpha)}{2x\ln{q}}.

(44)

The maximal increment of entanglement entropy between
t
&
t+1
is
4\ln(q)
, irrespective of
\kappa_{t}

Gopalakrishnan and Lamacraft 2019
. This gives us another metric to monitor entanglement growth as a fraction of the maximum, defined as:

\Delta S_{A}^{\alpha}(t)=\frac{S_{A}^{\alpha}(t)-S_{A}^{\alpha}(t-1)}{4\ln(q)}.

(45)

Note that
\tr(C_{x}C_{x}^{\dagger})=1
for all cases, but when
mm^{\dagger}=\mathbb{I}
,
C_{x}C_{x}^{\dagger}
reduces to
\mathbb{I}_{x}/q^{x}
. This corresponds to the exactly solvable MPS
Piroli et al. 2020
and the entanglement velocity
v^{(\alpha)}_{E}
is found to be unity in all cases. The value of
v_{E}
for an arbitrary
m
doesnot reach the same for a finite case.

Figure 4:
Entanglement velocities (for initial state
\ket{0}^{\otimes L}
) and mixing rates for a collection of 1000 ensembles for
(a)
qubits and
(c)
qutrits case along with the correlation between
\mathbb{E}_{u_{i},v_{i}}(v_{E})
and
\mathbb{E}_{u_{i},v_{i}}(|\lambda_{1}|)
for
(b)
qubits and
(d)
qutrits for the same data set.

To calculate bipartite entanglement within our system for the
A|\bar{A}
partition, we may alternatively generate the state
\mathbb{U}(t)\ket{\psi_{init}}
evaluating
S_{A}^{\alpha}(t)
numerically. This approach gives us the same results in the regime
t\leq t^{*}
as those obtained via the
C
operator, and while allowing us to probe further for larger
t
where the factorization given by Eq.
40
is no longer valid. Following this approach for
\alpha=2
we see in Fig.
3
that the entanglement builds up rapidly, and saturates. For the case of unitary
m
, the entanglement growth follows along the maximum rate of
v_{E}=1
, irrespective of the entangling power
e_{P}(U)
. Comparing this to the case where
m
is randomly selected and non-unitary, the entanglement growth increases with increasing
e_{P}(U)
, edging closer to the case where
v_{E}=1
(compare Fig
3

(b)
and
(d)
). Taking examples of
N=8,12\&16
we can see (in Fig
3

(a)
and
(c)
) the different time-steps at which saturation occurs. Irrespective of
m
, the entangelement saturates at the same values for each
N
, with system size determining the saturation value. It is just that for random
m
is takes longer to reach there, depending on
e_{P}(U)
.

Therefore, the role of single operator
U^{\prime}
is most apparent when
t\leq t^{*}
, for random
m
and to study that our approach is to create
C_{\kappa_{t}}
operators with increasing values of
t
assuming that the underlying finite system is large enough to satisfy the pre-saturation conditions.

III.1
Dependence on Mixing Rate

In this section we present one of the main results of our manuscript, describing how the mixing rate influences entanglement dynamics as reflected in the measure of entanglement velocity. We find that for an ensemble of operators
U^{\prime}
constrained with a fixed entangling power there still exists a range of values for both
v^{(\alpha)}_{E}
and
\Delta S_{A}^{\alpha}
at a moderate time step
t
. This variation is found upon constructing the circuit operator
C_{\kappa_{t}}
for all members of the
U^{\prime}
ensemble and evaluating the entanglement velocity using equations Eq.
40
and Eq.
44
, with the same initial state. We shall demonstrate that this variability is a result of local unitaries varying the mixing rate of the system while keeping the entangling power of individual gates unchanged. Finally, we find that it is the mixing rate which drives the growth of entanglement, revealing the subtle but significant role of local unitaries in quantum circuit dynamics.

It is known that local unitaries have the tendency to alter the entanglement generating capabilities of a circuit, without affecting the
e_{P}(U)
metric
Jonnadula et al. 2020
;
Jonnadula et al. 2017
, but using a circuit of dual unitary operators allows us to explicitly state that the effect of local unitaries is manifested by their influence on the mixing rate. Note that
\lambda_{1}
which determines the mixing rate is derived from the expression in Eq.
22
, which takes a solvable form only for the class of
U\in\mathcal{DU}(q)
.

For generating our results, we begin with an operator
U\in\mathcal{DU}(q)
for
q=2
and
3
, having a specific
e_{P}(U)
. We then create the ensemble
\{U^{\prime}\}_{u,v}
of operators, with each member leading to a distinct matrix
M_{+}
, and an associated value of
|\lambda_{1}|
. We have from Fig.
1

(b)
and
(d)
that increasing the value of
e_{P}(U)
inversely affects the average over
|\lambda_{1}|
, however to observe its impact on
v_{E}^{(\alpha)}
we construct the corresponding
C_{\kappa_{t}}
operators at
t=5
for each member of the ensemble, and calculate
v_{E}^{(\alpha=2)}(\kappa_{t})
using Eq.
44
. This process is repeated for a selection of
U
giving a range of
e_{P}(U)
values extending to unity for
U\in\mathcal{DU}(3)
for the case when
U\in\mathcal{U}_{2}(3)
, and limited to
e_{P}(U)=0.66
for
U\in\mathcal{DU}(2)
. Having prepared the ensemble
\{U^{\prime}\}_{u,v}
and calculating the values of
v_{E}^{(\alpha)}
along with
|\lambda_{1}|
, we plot them of a single graph to give Fig.
4

(a)
and
(c)
. We can clearly observe that for increasing values of
e_{P}(U)
, the points denoting ensemble members drift towards higher
v_{E}
and lower
|\lambda_{1}|
values.

To quantify our finding in a more systematic manner we next plot the ensemble averages of the
v_{E}^{(2)}
and
|\lambda_{1}|
that we obtained for the aforementioned ensemble
\{U^{\prime}\}_{u,v}
for each
e_{P}(U)
. This variation is presented in Figures Fig.
4

(b)
and
(d)
. While
e_{P}(U)
does not have an explicit mention in the figure, each point in both the graphs represent an entire ensemble
\{U^{\prime}\}_{u,v}
, having a fixed value of
e_{P}(U^{\prime})=e_{P}(U)
. In the overall picture, we find a monotonic increase in the entanglement velocity
v_{E}^{(2)}
upon the decrease in
|\lambda_{1}|
. A reduction of
\mathbb{E}(|\lambda_{1}|)
from
0.9
to
0.6
is accompanied by a threefold increase in
\mathbb{E}(v_{E}^{(2)})
for qubit case. This increase is sustained to
\mathbb{E}_{u,v}|\lambda_{1}|=0
for the Bernoulli class in the case of qutrits, having
e_{P}(U)=1
. Here even a random
m
gives a high average
\mathbb{E}(v_{E}^{(2)})
of
0.8
for the qutrit Bernoulli case when
\lambda_{1}=0
for all ensemble members.

At this point, it becomes important to stress that while our results till now consider the variation of two specific metrics viz.
\mathbb{E}(v_{E})
and
\mathbb{E}(\lambda_{1})
with respect to each other, entangling power still plays an implicit role in the overall picture, beyond affecting the mixing rate. Take the case when
e_{P}(U)=0
, there is no entanglement buildup, leading to vanishing entanglement velocity. In the following section we elaborate on the role of entangling power, by presenting results for the explicit buildup of entanglement with time over the ensemble
\{U^{\prime}\}_{u,v}
, taking circuit operators
C_{t}
with increasing
t
.

III.2
Role of Entangling Power

Figure 5:
Growth of Renyi entropy, order
\alpha=2
, for the
(a)
qubit case and
(b)
qutrit case with increasing time steps
t
of the operator
C_{2t}
. The result is an ensemble average of 100 different initializations for the operator
U^{\prime}
with the same randomly selected state. The shaded region gives an area wherein the average may lie for any arbitrary
e_{P}(U)
.

The correlation between entanglement velocity and mixing rate of the system implies that entanglement generation is an inherent feature of ergodic dynamics. Here entangling power
e_{P}(U)
plays a crucial role, as it already influences the mixing rate as seen in Eq.
35
, and it is also known to lower bound the entanglement growth in a brickwall circuit
\mathbb{U}

Foligno and Bertini 2023
. The same type of lower bound appears for our setup as well, and upon calculation we shall see the role of entangling power.

Taking
U^{\prime}
as the building block of the circuit along with the local random unitaries we use the folded tensor notation to describe
\mathbb{E}_{u,v}\tr((C_{\kappa_t}C^{\dagger}_{\kappa_t})^2)
as
\mathcal{P}_{\kappa_{t}}
given here for
\kappa_{t}=4
as:

\mathcal{P}_{\kappa_{t}}=\,\,\,\hbox to158.15pt{\vbox to82.46pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 23.30487pt\lower 2.58763pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\par{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 48.23 6.89 L 124.02 82.68}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\par{}{{}}{}

(46)

where we have the averaged gate:

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(47)

Here
\otimes_{r}
denotes the tensor product over copies of
U^{\prime}
and
{U^{\prime}}^{*}
stacked over each other, obtained on folding the diagram
\tr((C_{\kappa_t}C^{\dagger}_{\kappa_t})^2)
and
P
denotes the projector operator arising out of averaging over haar random unitaries. The states
\ket{\circlew}
and
\ket{\squarew}
are given as:

\displaystyle\ket{\circlew}=\frac{1}{q}\sum\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}\ket{ijkl},

(48)

\displaystyle\ket{\squarew}=\frac{1}{q}\sum\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\ket{ijkl}.

(49)

\bar{S}_{A}^{2}(t)
represents the average entanglement generated by an ensemble of operators
U^{\prime}
and corresponding
C^{\prime}_{\kappa_{t}}
and is equal to
\mathbb{E}_{u,v}\big[-2\ln(\tr[({C'}_{\kappa_t}{C'}_{\kappa_t}^{\dagger})^2])\big]
. By Jensen’s inequality, we have for a convex function like
-2\ln(x)
that
\mathbb{E}(f(x))\leq f(\mathbb{E}(x))
, implying:

\bar{S}_{A}^{2}(t)\geq-2\ln[\mathbb{E}_{u,v}\Big(\tr({C'}_{\kappa_t}{C'}_{\kappa_t}^{\dagger})^{2}\Big)\Big].

(50)

The RHS depends on
\mathcal{P}_{\kappa_{t}}
which can be obtained analytically for small
\kappa_{t}
(see Appendix
B.2
for details) and serves as the lower bound for entanglement growth.

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{1}=\frac{c}{q};\,\,\,c=\frac{\tr((mm^{\dagger})^2)}{q}\in[1,q]\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{2}=\frac{1}{q^{2}}+\frac{2\chi}{q^{2}}\sqrt{q^{2}-1}+\frac{2\chi^{2}}{q^{2}}(q^{2}(1-e_{P})-1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{3}\leq\frac{1}{q^{3}}+\frac{2\chi}{q^{3}}\sqrt{q^{2}-1}+\frac{2\chi^{2}}{q^{3}}(q^{2}(1-e_{P})-1)+

\displaystyle\Bigg(\chi^{2}\frac{q+c}{q+1}\sqrt{\frac{\eta^{3}(e_{p})(q^{2}-1)}{q^{2}}}\Bigg)

here
\chi=(c-1)/\sqrt{q^{2}-1}\in\Big[0,\sqrt{\frac{q-1}{q+1}}\Big]
is a quantity necessarily smaller than unity and
\eta(e_{P})
is rapidly decaying function on increasing
e_{P}
being unity at
e_{P}=0
.

\eta(e_{P})=(1-e_{P})^{2}+\frac{e_{P}^{2}}{q^{2}-1}

(51)

\mathcal{P}_{3}
is the first non-trivial term expressed as an inequality. Upon increasing
x>3
for
\mathcal{P}_{x}
we get an expression involving higher powers of
\chi
. The term with
\eta(e_{P})
also has the highest power of
\chi
, and is of a negligible value for even moderately high
e_{P}
. Having the expression we may extract the factor
1/q^{x}
from
\mathcal{P}_{x}
to give
\mathcal{P}_{x}=p_{x}/q^{x}
.

\implies-2\ln(\mathcal{P}_x)=2x\ln(q)-\ln(p_x)\leq\bar{S}_{A}^{2}(t).

(52)

The lower bound for average entanglement velocity is obtained by dividing the above expression by the maximum value, given by
2x\ln(q)
:

\bar{v}_{E}^{(2)}(x)\geq 1-\frac{\ln(p_x)}{2x\ln(q)}.

(53)

The term subtracted from unity has its numerator as logarithm of a summation with terms containing increasing power of
\chi
, while it is being divided by linearly increasing
2x\ln(q)
factor. Hence, with each successive
x
, the entanglement velocity bound edges closer to unity. For the infinite case in terms of space and time, it is known that the value of
v_{E}(t)
does approach unity
\forall U\in\mathcal{DU}(q)
at
t\rightarrow\infty
, assuming sufficient entangling power associated with the gates
U
forming the circuit
Foligno and Bertini 2023
. This result holds for an arbitrary set of local unitaries at each space time point. Our setup for the finite case is an example of this, with the local unitaries
u
and
v
mounted upon
U^{\prime}
in effect a two-site invariant collection of local-unitaries, thereby explaining our observations. A check on the expression is also provided, as taking
c=q
(i.e. the case of unitary
m
) reduces each
p_{x}
to
1
, thereby making the lower bound equal to unity
\forall x
. Further, taking
e_{P}=0
for a typical value of
c
gives a negative lower bound, satisfying the zero entanglement growth observed for the same situation.

Figure 6:
Variation of Scott Measure for
L=12,r=6
, for
(a)
qubits, with initial state given by
\ket{0}^{\otimes L}
i.e.
m=diag(1,0)
,
(b)
qutrits, with initial state given by random
m
, having non-zero value of Scott Measure, with ensemble size for
\{\mathbb{U}\}_{U^{\prime}}
= 100

While
e_{P}(U)
sets a lower bound, the actual on-average entanglement growth significantly exceeds those. In the figures Fig.
5

(a)
and
(b)
we have calculated the entanglement values by selecting an operator
U
having a specific value of
e_{P}
, and then generating an ensemble of operators
U^{\prime}
by selecting matrices
u
and
v
from the haar random distribution (see Eq.
21
). Following this we construct the
C_{2t}
operator for increasing values of
t
and the final result is the ensemble average over all operators. This plot helps us see the actual buildup of entanglement and its dependence on entangling power. In this setting the entanglement growth is found to be dependent on
e_{P}(U^{\prime})
and scales positively with it upon increasing
t
. Our results align with the theoretical calculations of Ref.
Foligno and Bertini 2023
as we see a gradual increase in entanglement increment
\Delta S_{A}^{\alpha}(t)
(see Fig.
5

(a)
and
(b)
) for each sucessive time step, as we take the operator
C_{x}
with larger spatial and temporal dimensions. These successive increments presumably take
v_{E}^{(2)}
to unity for the infinite case.

IV
Multipartite Entanglement

We have observed for the bipartite case that increasing entangling power of the single gate, on-average, increases the entanglement velocity of the system over an ensemble of
U^{\prime}
operators. Now we consider the multipartite aspects of the generated entanglement structure and the role of single gate entangling power. We shall first discuss measures of multipartite entanglement and then we shall establish the relation between entangling power of a single gate and the overall multipartite entanglement generated within the time evolved state.

IV.1
Measures for Multipartite Entanglement

Table 1:
Table with all entanglement measures for the final time evolved state in comparison to the same for the AME state.

Measure

L=8,d=2

L=12,d=2

L=8,d=3

While there are several measures for bipartite entanglement, an exact apriori specification for multipartite entanglement is not well defined in the state picture. Infact, most multipartite measures are obtained by averaging the bipartite entanglement over a given set of bipartitions. A simple example of that is the Mayer-Wallach
Q
measure, which is given as:

Q(\psi)=2\Big(1-\frac{1}{L}\sum_{k=1}^{L}\tr(\rho_k^2)\Big),

(54)

where
\rho_{k}
represents the density matrix obtained after tracing out the
k
th qudit. This measure calculates the average purity by iteratively tracing out each qubit and computing the mean across all single-qubit reductions. It may be generalised by the Scott Measure, taking the average over all possible
r
sized bipartitions
Scott 2004
.

Q_{r}(\psi)=\frac{q^{r}}{q^{r}-1}\Big(1-\frac{1}{{}^{L}C_{r}}\sum_{|S|=r}\tr(\rho_S^2)\Big).

(55)

In our analysis, we calculate the Scott measure at successive time steps using quantum circuits
\mathbb{U}(t)
with
e_{P}(U)
as a parameter, applied to initial states characterized by the matrix
m
. We denote this time-dependent measure as
S_{MP}^{r}(\psi(t))
. For our calculations, we set
r=L/2
, i.e. maximum partition size for the measure, and present results for both qubit and qutrit case. We examine two conditions: cases with zero initial value (Fig.
6
(a)) and non-zero initial values (Fig.
6
(b)), with the final result being an ensemble average over
\{\mathbb{U}\}_{U^{\prime}}
. The first case corresponds to using a diagonal matrix
m=\text{diag}(1,0)
, resulting in initial state
\ket{0}^{\otimes L}
while the latter employs a matrix
m
with random entries. Our results demonstrate that multipartite entanglement grows at a rate that scales directly with the entangling power of the constituent operators, regardless of the initial value. Moreover, we observe that when the measure reaches saturation, it approaches close to unity in both cases. Incase of our results, for
L=12
, and
q=2
the saturation value is
0.983
, and for
q=3
it is
0.998
. While circuits composed of operators with lower entangling power require longer time scales to achieve saturation, they ultimately reach the same high value.

Figure 7:
Comparison of entanglement via Von-Neumann entropy at each bond of the time evolved state with its corresponding maximum value.

This feature of the time evolved state where the value of Scott Measure for
r=L/2
approaches unity indicates that the state is close to an AME state. As a preliminary check we evaluate the Von-Neumann entropy
S_{VN}(i)
of the obtained state at each bond
i
, comparing it with the theoretical maximum for the resulting size of subsystems. This max. value at bond index
i
, using linearized entropy is given as
1-1/\text{min}(q^{i},q^{L-i})
. The results are shown in Figure
7
. A similar calculation for the AME state would lead to an overlap with the maximum bounds. While this feature of the time-evolved state is approximately similar to that of an AME state, the actual similarity between the two cannot be ascertained by this measure alone, as for the state to be near AME there must be a near maximization of multipartite entanglement. To quantify the similarity to an AME state, we consider another measure termed as the AME-GME metric
V. and S. 2024
. When considering states showing many-body entanglement, we define a genuinely multipartite entangled (GME) state as one where each qubit is entangled with the remaining system. This implies that across any possible bipartition
A/\bar{A}
the linearized entropy gives a non-zero value. The GME state is termed as an AME state, if the entanglement across all possible partitions is maximum. Taking this into account, a measure can be defined as follows
V. and S. 2024
:

E(\psi)=\Pi_{s=1}^{\lfloor L/2\rfloor}\Pi_{k=1}^{r_{s}}\frac{q^{s}}{q^{s}-1}\Big(1-\tr([\rho_{k}^{s}]^2)\Big).

(56)

Here,
r_{s}
denotes the number of all possible partitions having
s
qudits.
s
can therefore take the values
\{1,2,3...,\lfloor L/2\rfloor\}
for a system with
L
qudits. The density matrix
\rho_{k}^{s}
denotes the
k
th example of a bipartition, involving
s
parties.
r_{s}
is therefore given by
{}^{L}C_{s}
.
The measure gives a non-zero value only under the condition that
\psi
is a GME state, as the product representing
E(\psi)
vanishes if any term of the product becomes zero corresponding to vanishing entanglement for a specific bipartition. The measure gives the maximum value of unity only in the case when
\psi
is AME.

In its original form the measure
E(\psi)
has the numerical value between zero and one, with a value closer to one indicating closeness to the AME state. Using the original form however, the value for our time-evolved state gives an anomalously low value around
0.1
. This can however be explained as each product term has a value
\leq 1
and
\geq 0.9
. Upon repeated multiplication this leads to the small anomalous value. To get around this, we modify the above measure by introducing a geometric mean at the end of all the products.

E_{GM}(\psi)=\Bigg(\Pi_{s=1}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\Pi_{k=1}^{r_{s}}\frac{q^{s}}{q^{s}-1}\Big(1-\tr([\rho_{k}^{s}]^2)\Big)\Bigg)^{1/M}.

Here
M=\sum_{s=1}^{\lfloor L/2\rfloor}r_{s}
, equal to the total number of product terms. As in the other examples of multipartite measures,
E_{GM}(\psi)
also represents an average over entanglement values upon all possible bipartitions and maybe used as a measure of multipartite entanglement. Infact, it gives a very similar variation to that of the Scott Measure for
r=\lfloor L/2\rfloor
, with the added feature that its non-vanishing value implies the presence of genuine multipartite entanglement.

Figure 8:
(a)
Multipartite entangling power
e_{P}^{Q_{r}}
, for
r=2
,
L=4
and
(b)
Operator-Space entangling power
e_{OS}
with time for the entire circuit (
L=4
), with local single gate entangling power
e_{P}(U)
as a parameter.

Following the geometric-mean averaged AME-GME measure, we again find high values, being
0.986
for
N=12
and
q=2
, while reaching
0.999
for the case when
q=3
. Both these numbers are a multiplication result of
{}^{12}C_{6}=924
different terms. In order to compare the closeness of our state with the AME State we consider yet another measure which is the average Von-Neumann entanglement entropy for all possible half system bipartitions. This measure gives the maximum value for an AME state, with the value being
L/2
, taking the base of logarithm to be the same as that of the local dimension. This measure is formally defined as:

\bar{S}_{VN}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{{}^{L}C_{L/2}}\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{q^{L/2}}\lambda_{j}\log_{q}(\lambda_{j})\Big)}{{}^{L}C_{L/2}}=\frac{L}{2},\text{if}\,\,\psi=\text{AME}

Note that the way in which the measure is defined makes it similar to the Scott measure, based on Von-Neumann instead of linearized entropy. As shown in Table
1
, the values for all measures are close to the maximum achieved by the AME state. Here, we explain another feature (taking example of
N=12,q=3
), wherein the Scott(
0.998/1
) and AME-GME measures (
0.999/1
) are much closer to their maximum values than the averaged Von-Neumann entropy (
5.54/6
). However, this value is an average over
q^{L/2}
different terms, implying that each
\lambda\log_{q}(\lambda)
term differs, on-average, by a small amount from the maximum. This deviation is of order
6*10^{-3}
for
L=12,q=3
and
0.049
for
L=8,q=2
. The difference from maximum underlines the sensitivity of measure
\bar{S}_{VN}
to distinguish between states having multipartite entanglement close to the maximum, and the actual AME state.

Another important feature upon observation of Table
1
is that the states furthest from AME are those corresponding to
L=8,d=2
and
L=12,d=2
, for which an AME state does not actually exist. While it is possible to create AME states for higher
q
using quantum circuits
Berta Casas 2025
, our approach provides an approximate state having high multipartite entanglement which maybe useful in the absence of an actual AME state.

IV.2
Multipartite Entangling Power

We find that the build-up of multipartite entanglement starting out for a initially separated state is dependent on the entangling powers of the constituent dual unitaries within the circuit. We now evaluate the entanglement generating capacity of the entire circuit from the multipartite perspective. For this we construct the circuits of increasing temporal depth, and check for the dependence on single gate entangling power.

For a multipartite measure
E
we can define the associated entangling power of the circuit
\mathbb{U}(t)
as:

e_{P}^{MP}(\mathbb{U})=\int d\mu_{n}(\psi_{1},...,\psi_{n})E\Big(\mathbb{U}\ket{\psi_1}\otimes...\otimes\ket{\psi_n}\Big),

(57)

where the measure
d\mu_{n}
represents
n
independent Haar random measures. With
E
being the Scott measure, the multipartite entangling power
e_{P}^{Q_{r}}
is given as
Scott 2004
:

e_{P}^{Q_{r}}(\mathbb{U})=\frac{q^{r}}{q^{r}-1}\Big(1-\frac{r!(r-L)!}{L!}\sum_{|S|=r}R_{S}(\mathbb{U})\Big),

(58)

where
R_{S}(U)
is given by the following expression:

R_{S}(\mathbb{U})=\mathcal{C}\tr[\mathbb{U}^{\otimes 2}\Big(\Pi_{i=1}^{L}P_{i,i+L}\Big)\mathbb{U}^{\dagger\otimes 2}\Big(\Pi_{i\in S}T_{i,i+L}\Big)\Bigg].

here
T_{ij}
is the SWAP operator between the
i
th and
j
th factors of
(C^{q})^{\otimes 2L}
, for
(0\leq i\leq L)
and
P_{ij}=(1+T_{ij})/2
. The constant of proportionality
\mathcal{C}
normalises the measure and is given as
\Big(2/q(q+1)\Big)^{n}
.

Using this working formula we calculate
e_{P}^{Q_{r}}(\mathbb{U}(t))
, for increasing values of
t
, with the different entangling powers
e_{P}(U)
of individual gates
U
as a parameter in Fig.
8

(a)
. Here we perform calculations only for the qubit case, with
L=4
given the computational requirements of implementing the
\mathbb{U}^{\otimes 2}
operation. We take
r=2
to obtain
e_{P}^{Q_{r}}
corresponding to the results of our previous section. We find that for all values of
e_{P}(U)
other than when the local operator is SWAP, there is an increase in the multipartite entangling power
e_{P}^{Q_{r=2}}(\mathbb{U}(t))
with time
t
. With higher values of
e_{P}(U)
,
e_{P}^{Q_{r}}
starts off with a high initial value at the first step, (
0.2\,\&\,0.6
for
e_{P}(U)=0.19\,\&\,0.56
respectively, compared to
0.0
for
e_{P}(U)=0.01
) and quickly reaches maximum saturation. Even for small values of
e_{P}(U)
(see case
e_{P}(U)=0.01
) the value of multipartite entangling power increases gradually, and it is only for SWAP that the latter is zero. The values of
e_{P}^{Q_{r=2}}
saturate at a value of around
0.7\,\&\,0.55
, when
e_{P}(U)=0.56\,\&\,0.19
indicating the mechanisms responsible for the delayed onset of the final state with high multipartite entanglement when
e_{P}(U)
is lower, as seen in the previous section.

Given the high multipartite entangling properties of the circuit
\mathbb{U}
, we consider another measure to analyse the associated dynamical nature. For this we use the recently introduced metric of operator space entangling power
Andreadakis et al. 2024
. It is defined as the average operator entanglement generated by a channel given by
\mathcal{E}[\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.04544pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.94 0 C 3.94 2.17 2.17 3.94 0 3.94 C -2.17 3.94 -3.94 2.17 -3.94 0 C -3.94 -2.17 -2.17 -3.94 0 -3.94 C 2.17 -3.94 3.94 -2.17 3.94 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}]=O^{\dagger}[\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.04544pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.94 0 C 3.94 2.17 2.17 3.94 0 3.94 C -2.17 3.94 -3.94 2.17 -3.94 0 C -3.94 -2.17 -2.17 -3.94 0 -3.94 C 2.17 -3.94 3.94 -2.17 3.94 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}]O
, for an ensemble of separable haar random operators defined for a specific bipartition
(a:b)
of the system, being acted upon by
\mathcal{E}
. It is therefore a metric defining the entanglement generating capability of
\mathbb{U}
in the operator space, when we take
\mathcal{E}_{t}[\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.04544pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.94 0 C 3.94 2.17 2.17 3.94 0 3.94 C -2.17 3.94 -3.94 2.17 -3.94 0 C -3.94 -2.17 -2.17 -3.94 0 -3.94 C 2.17 -3.94 3.94 -2.17 3.94 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}]=\mathbb{U}^{\dagger}(t)[\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.04544pt\lower-3.04544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 3.94 0 C 3.94 2.17 2.17 3.94 0 3.94 C -2.17 3.94 -3.94 2.17 -3.94 0 C -3.94 -2.17 -2.17 -3.94 0 -3.94 C 2.17 -3.94 3.94 -2.17 3.94 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}]\mathbb{U}(t)
. Following this definition the metric is given as:

e_{OS}(\mathbb{U}(t))=\mathbb{E}_{x_{a},y_{b}}E\Big(\mathcal{E}_{t}(x_{a}\otimes y_{b})\Big).

(59)

For our case, since the time evolution is unitary we use the notation centered on
\mathbb{U}
instead of
\mathcal{E}
. The above form maybe expressed in terms of subsystem non-commutativity measures, which results into the working expression for a half-system bipartition
Andreadakis et al. 2024
:

\displaystyle e_{OS}(\mathbb{U}(t))=1-\Big(1-\frac{E(\mathbb{U})(t)}{E({\tilde{S}})}\Big)^{2}-\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\

\displaystyle\Big(1-\frac{E(\mathbb{U}(t)\tilde{S})}{E({\tilde{S}})}\Big)^{2}-\frac{2}{q}\frac{E(\mathbb{U}(t))}{E(\tilde{S})}\frac{E(\mathbb{U}(t)\tilde{S})}{E(\tilde{S})}.

Here
\tilde{S}
denotes the SWAP operator exchanging the equal subsystem spaces. The value of
e_{OS}(\mathbb{U})
for half-system bipartition extends from zero for identity operator
\mathbb{I}_{d}
to a maximum value of
1-2/(d+1)
, where
d=2^{L}
. When
e_{OS}
reaches its maximum value the channel
\mathcal{E}_{t}
transforms every instance of the product operator
x_{a}\otimes y_{b}
to a haar-random operator having the dimensionality of the total system. Therefore in the case of unitary evolution,
e_{OS}(\mathbb{U})
describes the scrambling properties of the circuit operator
\mathbb{U}
.

In our case, following along the lines of multipartite entangling power, we plot
e_{OS}(\mathbb{U}(t))
as a function of
t
in Fig.
8

(b)
taking the local entangling power
e_{P}(U)
as a parameter. With
L=4
the maximum value for
e_{OS}(\mathbb{U})
is
0.88
, which is reached at
t\sim 10
for moderate values of
e_{P}(U)
. With the same system size,
L=4
we observe that the value of
e_{OS}(\mathbb{U}(t))
increases with
t
, and the rate of growth is also found to be dependent on the individual gate
U
via
e_{P}(U)
. Except for the circuit being constructed of 2-qudit SWAP operators, the measure build up in time, indicating that a circuit of higher depth may match the scrambling properties of a circuit made up of strongly entangling individual operators. The value of
e_{OS}(\mathbb{U})
remains zero for the SWAP operator, indicating that important role of
e_{P}(U)
, a feature also observed for the multipartite entangling power.

V
Relation with Spin Chains

Figure 9:
Variation of entangling power with time for different classes of Ising Model.
L=6
and
r=3
for the measure
e_{P}^{Q_{3}}
.

In the previous section we studied the multipartite entangling properties of the entire brickwall circuit along with their scrambling capabilities, and found them to show similar qualitative dependence on the entangling powers of individual gates while also similarly scaling with time. Given this similarity we shall now explore the multipartite entangling powers for physical systems corresponding to different classes of dynamical and scrambling behaviours. This relation is not generic however, with the simplest counter-example being that of the SWAP operator, which is non-local and has maximal scrambling capacity
Claeys and Lamacraft 2020
, yet has no entangling capability. We take the prototypical example of a transverse field ising model on qubits to generate the different classes of models ranging from chaotic to Anderson localised. While it is expected that the generated entanglement over an ensemble of initial states increases with the scrambling nature of associated time evolution, explicitly calculating the metric using equation Eq.
58
gives a clear separation between the different classes.

The general form of the Hamiltonian for transverse field Ising model is given as:

H=-\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\sigma^{z}_{i}\sigma^{z}_{i+1}-\sum_{i=1}^{L}(h\sigma^{z}_{i}+g_{i}\sigma^{x}_{i}),

(60)

where
h
and
g
represent the longitudinal and transverse Ising fields. Using these parameters we can have the Ising model corresponding to the following different dynamical classes:

•

Integrable Model
:
h=0
and
g_{i}=1
, for all the lattice sites. This model is exactly solvable following via Jordan-Wigner mapping to a free fermionic system.

•

Non-Integrable (Chaotic) Model
:
h=0.5
and
g_{i}=1.05
,
\forall i\in[1,L]
. The presence of a longitudinal field makes the hamiltonian interacting and chaotic, with the nearest neighbour spacing being described by Wigner-Dyson distribution
Shukla et al. 2022
.

•

Anderson Localisation
:
h=0
and
g_{i}
is selected randomly for each site from the uniform distribution between
[-10,10]
, this being the prototypical example for a model showing localisation.

•

Many-Body Localisation
:
h=0.5
and
g_{i}
is again randomly selected for each site using the uniform random distribution such that
g_{i}\in[-10,10]
. It is the interacting version of the Anderson localised model
Suntajs et al. 2020
.

Having defined the models, we obtain the unitary propagator
\tilde{U}
corresponding to each of these and then calculate the multipartite entangling power using equation Eq.
58
, for
L=6
. We find that for the models corresponding to localisation the multipartite entangling power remains suppressed and attains a lower value compared to the integrable and chaotic examples, with the MBL phase showing still higher saturation compared to Anderson. Particularly a difference is also observed between integrable and nonintegrable (chaotic) versions not found in the operator space entangling power, owing to the requirement of local unitaries with a 2-unitary design. Such operators donot register conserved quantities in local Hilbert spaces corresponding to the integrable model. However the measure
e_{P}^{MP}(\tilde{U})
is accurately generated by 1-unitary designs which factorise into the single particle space, differentiating the dynamics from non-integrable model
Andreadakis et al. 2024
.

VI
Summary and Conclusions

In this work we have considered information scrambling and entanglement generation within a finite system starting out with a generic initial state being transformed by a brickwall circuit composed of dual unitary operators. Under time evolution of this kind the space-time correlation function serves as an effective diagnostic of ergodic properties. When the correlation function is non-zero, i.e. on the lightcone connecting the space-time points, it displays constant, decaying, or oscillating behavior. The exact evaluation of the correlation function and its associated behaviour occurs in terms of a unital channel, dependent upon the individual operator
U
used in constructing the aforementioned circuit. This observation has prompted us to explore the role of individual operators in other dynamical aspects like scrambling and entanglement buildup.

The property of individual operators most relevant to our discussion is that of entangling power. Apart from the direct relation to entanglement generation, the entangling power beyond a specific bound guarantees the circuit to be ergodically mixing. Moreover using a metric in terms of
|\lambda_{1}|
to quantify this rate we find that over several ensembles of local unitary equivalent
U
the average mixing rate scales positively with the entangling power. In relation to the bipartite case we use the metric of entanglement velocity to analyse the buildup of entanglement and find that it correlates with the mixing rate, increasing with increased mixing. Taking the mixing rate allows us to consider the role of local unitaries in the dynamic properties of the circuit. In the overall picture we find that the entangling powers sets system dependent lower bounds in terms of both the ergodic nature and entanglement buildup. This exact calculation of the mixing rate is not possible for an arbitrary circuit and thus using dual unitaries we can put forward the example of a system where increased ergodicity implies greater entanglement.

Moving on, we find that the entanglement structure in the time evolved state also has near maximum values for multipartite metrics of entanglement such as the Scott Measure. For circuits with
U
of higher entangling power the growth rate of multipartite entanglement is higher. This implies a faster convergence to the final state with near-maximum multipartite measure. The time evolved state at saturation, with these high measures are indeed found to be close to the corresponding AME state if it exists. In other cases we still end up with a state with high multipartite entanglement which maybe useful in the implementation of quantum computing protocols.

Finally we evaluate a few metrics defined for the entire circuit itself against increasing temporal depth, with the entangling power of gates being a varied parameter. The first of this is the metric of multipartite entangling power based on the Scott measure. Using this metric we observe that a circuit with greater depth has a greater capacity to generate multipartite entanglement, and that for a given depth taking individual operators of greater entangling power again enhances this capacity. To relate this entanglement generating capability with the scrambling nature of the dynamics, we evaluate the operator space entangling power for the whole circuit, which again shows a very similar qualitative behaviour. The operator space entangling power reaches its upper bounds at relatively small time steps if the entangling power is high. For both of these circuit measures there is a significant growth observed for a moderate number of time steps, except when the individual operators have vanishing entangling powers by being local unitary equivalent to the SWAP operator.

In the end, we consider different variants of the transverse field ising model from the localising to thermal regime and based on their tendency for information scrambling, and find them to have their multipartite entangling powers in the same order as their scrambling rates. This observation serves as an example of a physical model having similar properties of our finite system under dual unitary dynamics.

To summarize, we conclude that the setup used in this dynamics serves as an example where the entangling nature correlates well with the ergodicity and the scrambling tendencies of the system. Moreover, the entangling power of the individual gates forming the circuit is a strong predictor of the associated dynamical features.
